So, while both the Freikorps and the German Socialist Party existed within the same country (Germany) and shared some ideological ground, they were separate entities. The Heptarchy refers to the period of Anglo-Saxon England from around 500 AD until the early 10th century when seven kingdoms (Northumbria, Mercia, East Anglia, Essex, Kent, Sussex, and Wessex) were in existence. These kingdoms eventually merged into a single unified kingdom under the rule of King Edgar the Peaceful in the late 960s. For example: - The Kingdom of Northumbria was effectively divided between Scotland and England by the mid 10th century. - Mercia's power declined in the late 9th century after being conquered by Wessex under Alfred the Great. - Kent, Essex, Sussex, and East Anglia were gradually absorbed into larger kingdoms like Wessex. The last of these Anglo-Saxon kingdoms to maintain a distinct identity before unification was Wessex itself. In 927 AD, King Edward the Elder of Wessex (son of Alfred the Great) annexed Mercia and Northumbria, effectively bringing most of England under his control. His son Athelstan then became the first king of all England in 927. So, if you are asking about when the Heptarchy system ended or when a specific kingdom within it was abolished, the answer would be around 927 AD with the unification of England under King Athelstan.

### Pair 343

**Question:**
What empire brought the practices of the most common religion in Southeast Asia?

**Ground Truth:**
Hephthalite Empire,

**Retrieved Answer:**
The empire that played a significant role in spreading Buddhism, which is now the most common religion in much of Southeast Asia, was the Maurya Empire, particularly under Emperor Ashoka (reigning from c. 268 to 232 BCE). However, if we are looking at an empire that had a broader and more lasting influence across Southeast Asia, it would be the spread through the South Asian empires and kingdoms following the decline of Maurya, as well as the introduction by Chinese, Thai, Khmer (Cambodian), and other regional powers. Buddhism was introduced to Southeast Asia via various routes. The earliest significant spread came from Indian missionaries and traders who established Buddhist communities in regions like Sri Lanka, Myanmar (Burma), Thailand, Cambodia, Laos, and Vietnam. The most influential empire after the Maurya that contributed significantly to this spread was the Gupta Empire of India, which flourished between the 3rd and 6th centuries CE. The Gupta period saw a revival and expansion of Buddhism in India, leading to further dissemination into Southeast Asia. Additionally, the Pagan Kingdom (also known as Bagan) in Myanmar played a crucial role in spreading Theravada Buddhism throughout mainland Southeast Asia during its peak from the 11th to the 13th centuries CE. The Pagan Empire's influence extended across much of what is now modern-day Myanmar and into neighboring regions. Therefore, while multiple empires contributed to the spread of Buddhism in Southeast Asia, the Maurya Empire under Ashoka was foundational, followed by later Indian kingdoms like the Gupta Empire, and regional powers such as the Pagan Kingdom.

### Pair 451

**Question:**
Which interpretation of the work of the writer of The Canon of Medicine was taught more in Islamic schools?

**Ground Truth:**
al-Razi

**Retrieved Answer:**
The question seems to be asking about which interpretation of Avicenna's (the author of "The Canon of Medicine") works was more commonly taught in Islamic educational institutions. Given that Avicenna is a central figure in Islamic philosophy and medicine, his work has been subject to various interpretations over the centuries. In Islamic schools, particularly madrasas, the predominant method of interpreting religious texts and philosophical works like those of Avicenna involves tafsir (exegesis) for religious texts and ta'wil for philosophical and mystical texts. Ta'wil is a hermeneutical approach that seeks to uncover the inner meaning or esoteric significance behind the literal text. For Avicenna's work, which includes both medical and philosophical content, his philosophical works were often subjected to ta'wil by scholars who sought deeper meanings beyond the surface level. This was because many of his ideas in philosophy and science were seen as challenging traditional Islamic thought and required careful interpretation to reconcile with established beliefs. Therefore, it can be inferred that the hermeneutic approach known as ta'wil would have been more commonly applied to Avicenna's philosophical works in Islamic schools, aiming to uncover deeper meanings while maintaining compatibility with Islamic teachings. This method allowed for a nuanced understanding of his complex ideas and their integration into the broader corpus of Islamic knowledge. However, it is important to note that this was not uniform across all Islamic educational institutions or scholars, as there were varying degrees of acceptance and interpretation of Avicenna's work depending on regional traditions and individual scholarly perspectives.

### Pair 465

**Question:**
Where is the origin of the river that Orlam clans crossed to migrate to Namibia?

**Ground Truth:**
Thaba Putsoa

**Retrieved Answer:**
The question asks about the origin of the river that the Orlam clans crossed during their migration to Namibia. The Orlam clans were part of a group of Khoekhoe people who migrated into Namibia in the early 19th century, and they are known to have crossed the Orange River (also called the Gariep River or simply the Oranje) as part of this movement. The Orange River originates in the Drakensberg mountains in Lesotho. It flows westward through South Africa before forming the border between Namibia and South Africa for a stretch, after which it continues into the Atlantic Ocean at Alexander Bay on the South African side. Therefore, the origin of the river that the Orlam clans crossed to migrate to Namibia is in Lesotho, specifically in the Drakensberg mountains.

However, by September 1914, this rapid advance had slowed significantly due to stiffening French resistance and the arrival of British Expeditionary Force (BEF) troops at Mons on August 23. The German offensive was initially stopped along the Marne River, specifically during the Battle of the Marne which took place from September 6-12, 1914. Article Three

The Constitution does not explicitly delegate the power to establish court rules of conduct directly to Congress in any single Article. For example, the Code of Conduct for United States Judges is established by the Judicial Conference of the United States, which operates under authority granted by Congress. So, while there isn't a single article that provides for establishing rules of conduct specifically, Article III in conjunction with Congressional legislation allows for such regulations to be put into place.

The Goods and Services Tax (GST) bill was passed in the Indian Parliament, which includes the Lok Sabha, on August 12, 2016. The Lok Sabha Speaker is elected by members of the Lok Sabha, so this date refers to when the GST bill was passed in the organization that elects its speaker. However, it's important to note that the process for implementing GST involved several stages and approvals: - The Constitution (122nd Amendment) Bill, 2014 was introduced in the Lok Sabha on December 19, 2014. - It was passed by the Lok Sabha on May 6, 2015. - It was then passed by the Rajya Sabha with some amendments on August 3, 2016. - The amended bill was again passed by the Lok Sabha on August 8, 2016. - Finally, it received presidential assent and became law on September 8, 2016. So while the specific date you asked about (GST bill passing in Lok Sabha) is August 8, 2016, the full process took place over a period from late 2014 to early 2017.
